<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other similarly named saints, see <a href="/wiki/List_of_saints_named_Teresa" title="List of saints named Teresa">List of saints named Teresa</a>.</div>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Thérèse of Lisieux</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">OCD</a></span> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">Thérèse de Lisieux</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">[te.ʁɛz də li.zjø]</a></span>), born <b>Marie Françoise-Thérèse Martin</b> (2 January 1873 – 30 September 1897), also known as <b>Saint Therese of the Child Jesus and the Holy Face</b> (<span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus et de la Sainte Face</i></span>), was a French <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a> <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Discalced Carmelite</a> <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nun</a> who is widely venerated in modern times. She is popularly known in English as the <b>Little Flower of Jesus</b>, or simply the <b>Little Flower</b>, and in French as <b><span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">la petite Thérèse</i></span></b> (little Thérèse).<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thérèse has been a highly influential model of sanctity for Catholics and for others because of the simplicity and practicality of her approach to the spiritual life. She is one of the most popular <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saints</a> in the history of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Flinn_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Flinn-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_X" title="Pope Pius X">Pope Pius X</a> called her "the greatest saint of modern times".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont19965_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont19965-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thérèse felt an early <a href="/wiki/Vocational_discernment_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Vocational discernment in the Catholic Church">call to religious life</a> and, after overcoming various obstacles, in 1888, at the early age of 15,<sup id="cite_ref-The_Life_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Life-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> she became a nun and joined two of her older sisters in the <a href="/wiki/Cloister" title="Cloister">cloistered</a> Carmelite community of <a href="/wiki/Lisieux" title="Lisieux">Lisieux</a>, <a href="/wiki/Normandy" title="Normandy">Normandy</a> (yet another sister, Céline, also later joined the order). After nine years as a Carmelite religious, having fulfilled various offices such as <a href="/wiki/Sacristan" title="Sacristan">sacristan</a> and assistant to the <a href="/wiki/Master_of_novices" class="mw-redirect" title="Master of novices">novice mistress</a>, in her last eighteen months in Carmel she fell into a <a href="/wiki/Dark_Night_of_the_Soul#In_Roman_Catholic_spirituality" title="Dark Night of the Soul">night of faith</a>, in which she is said to have felt Jesus was absent and been tormented by doubts that God existed. Thérèse died at the age of 24 from <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a>.
</p><p>Her <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> was 3 October from 1927 until it was moved in 1969 to 1 October.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Thérèse is well known throughout the world, with the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_(Lisieux)" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St. Thérèse (Lisieux)">Basilica of Lisieux</a> being the second most popular place of pilgrimage in France after <a href="/wiki/Lourdes" title="Lourdes">Lourdes</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_background">Family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg/180px-Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="269" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg/270px-Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a2/Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="314" data-file-height="470" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Z%C3%A9lie_Martin_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Z%C3%A9lie_Martin" class="mw-redirect" title="Zélie Martin">Zélie Martin</a>, mother of Therese</div></div></div>
<p>Therese was born on Rue Saint-Blaise, in <a href="/wiki/Alen%C3%A7on" title="Alençon">Alençon</a>, France on January 2, 1873, and was the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Louis_Martin_and_Marie-Az%C3%A9lie_Gu%C3%A9rin" title="Louis Martin and Marie-Azélie Guérin">Marie-Azélie Guérin</a> (usually called Zélie), and <a href="/wiki/Louis_Martin_and_Marie-Az%C3%A9lie_Gu%C3%A9rin" title="Louis Martin and Marie-Azélie Guérin">Louis Martin</a> who was a jeweler and watchmaker.<sup id="cite_ref-Louis_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Louis-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Both her parents were devout Catholics who would eventually become the first (and to date only) married couple canonized together by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a> (by Pope Francis in 2015).
</p><p>Louis had tried to become a <a href="/wiki/Canon_regular" title="Canon regular">canon regular</a>, wanting to enter the <a href="/wiki/Great_St_Bernard_Hospice" title="Great St Bernard Hospice">Great St Bernard Hospice</a>, but had been refused because he did not know <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>. Zélie, possessed of a strong, active temperament, wished to serve the sick, and had also considered entering <a href="/wiki/Consecrated_life" title="Consecrated life">consecrated life</a>, but the <a href="/wiki/Prioress" class="mw-redirect" title="Prioress">prioress</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Canonesses_regular" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonesses regular">canonesses regular</a> of the Hôtel-Dieu in Alençon had discouraged her outright.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199614_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199614-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Disappointed, Zélie learned <a href="/wiki/Point_d%27Alen%C3%A7on" class="mw-redirect" title="Point d&#39;Alençon">lacemaking</a> instead. She excelled in it and set up her own business on Rue Saint-Blaise at age 22.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis and Zélie met in early 1858 and married on July 13 of that same year at the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_Notre-Dame_d%27Alen%C3%A7on" title="Basilica of Notre-Dame d&#39;Alençon">Basilica of Notre-Dame d'Alençon</a>. At first they decided to live as brother and sister in a <a href="/wiki/Josephite_marriage" title="Josephite marriage">perpetual continence</a>, but when a confessor discouraged them in this, they changed their lifestyle and had nine children. From 1867 to 1870, they lost 3 infants and five-year-old Hélène. All five of their surviving daughters became nuns. In addition to Therese, they were:
</p>
<ul><li>Marie (February 22, 1860, a Carmelite in Lisieux, in religion Sister Marie of the Sacred Heart, d. January 19, 1940),</li>
<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Agnes_of_Jesus_(religious)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Agnes of Jesus (religious) (page does not exist)">Pauline</a> (September 7, 1861, a Carmelite in Lisieux, in religion Mother Agnes of Jesus, d. July 28, 1951),</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9onie_Martin" title="Léonie Martin">Léonie</a> (June 3, 1863, a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Visitation_of_Holy_Mary" title="Order of the Visitation of Holy Mary">Visitandine</a> at <a href="/wiki/Caen" title="Caen">Caen</a>, in religion Sister Françoise-Thérèse, d. June 16, 1941), and</li>
<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Genevieve_of_the_Holy_Face&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Genevieve of the Holy Face (page does not exist)">Céline</a> (April 28, 1869, a Carmelite in Lisieux, in religion Sister Geneviève of the Holy Face, d. February 25, 1959).</li></ul>
<p>"A dreamer and brooder, an idealist and romantic, [the father] gave touching and naïve pet names [to his daughters]: Marie was his 'diamond', Pauline his 'noble pearl', Céline 'the bold one'. But Therese was his 'little queen', to whom all treasures belonged."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres195941–42_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres195941–42-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Zélie was so successful in manufacturing lace that by 1870 Louis had sold his watchmaking shop to a nephew and handled the traveling and bookkeeping end of his wife's lacemaking business.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_infancy">Birth and infancy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Birth and infancy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se,_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg/220px-Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg/330px-Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg/440px-Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se%2C_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3056" data-file-height="2038" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Maison_natale_de_Sainte_Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se,_maison_de_Louis_et_Z%C3%A9lie_Martin.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Rue Saint-Blaise's house at Alençon: The family home and Therese's birthplace</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_Martin_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Louis_Martin_1.jpg/180px-Louis_Martin_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="276" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Louis_Martin_1.jpg/270px-Louis_Martin_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Louis_Martin_1.jpg/360px-Louis_Martin_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="471" data-file-height="723" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis_Martin_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Louis Martin, father of Therese</div></div></div>
<p>Soon after her birth in January 1873, the outlook for the survival of Therese Martin was uncertain. Because of her frail condition, she was entrusted to a <a href="/wiki/Wet_nurse" title="Wet nurse">wet nurse</a>, Rose Taillé, who had already nursed two of the Martin children.<sup id="cite_ref-Society_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Society-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Rose had her own children and could not live with the Martins, so Therese was sent to live with her in the <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr"><a href="/wiki/Bocage" title="Bocage">bocage</a></i></span> forests of <a href="/wiki/Semall%C3%A9" title="Semallé">Semallé</a>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Porche_basilique.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Porche_basilique.jpeg/180px-Porche_basilique.jpeg" decoding="async" width="180" height="232" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Porche_basilique.jpeg/270px-Porche_basilique.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Porche_basilique.jpeg/360px-Porche_basilique.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="1982" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Porche_basilique.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The basilica of Alençon, where Therese was baptized</div></div></div>
<p>On 2 April 1874, when she was 15 months old, she returned to Alençon where her family surrounded her with affection. "I hear the baby calling me <i>Mama!</i> as she goes down the stairs. On every step, she calls out <i>Mama!</i> and if I don't respond every time, she remains there without going either forward or back." (Madame Martin to Pauline, 21 November 1875) She was educated in a very Catholic environment, including <a href="/wiki/Mass_(liturgy)" title="Mass (liturgy)">Mass</a> attendance at 5:30<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>a.m., the strict observance of fasts, and prayer to the rhythm of the liturgical year. The Martins also practiced charity, visiting the sick and elderly and welcoming the occasional vagabond to their table. Even if she was not the model little girl, her sisters later portrayed, Therese was very responsive to this education. She played at being a nun. Described as generally a happy child, she also manifested other emotions, and often cried: "Céline is playing with the little one with some bricks […] I have to correct poor baby who gets into frightful tantrums when she can't have her own way. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199624_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199624-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> She rolls in the floor in despair believing all is lost. Sometimes she is so overcome she almost chokes. She's a nervous child, but she is very good, very intelligent, and remembers everything."<sup id="cite_ref-Society_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Society-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> At 22, Therese, then a Carmelite, admitted: "I was far from being a perfect little girl".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher199319_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher199319-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 1865 Zélie had complained of breast pain and in December 1876 a doctor told her of the seriousness of the tumour. Feeling death was imminent, Madame Martin had written to Pauline in spring 1877, "You and Marie will have no difficulties with her upbringing. Her disposition is so good. She is a chosen spirit."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In June 1877 she left for <a href="/wiki/Lourdes" title="Lourdes">Lourdes</a> hoping to be cured, but the miracle did not happen: "The Mother of God has not healed me because my time is up, and because God wills me to repose elsewhere than on the earth."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> On 28 August 1877, Zélie died, aged 45. Her funeral was conducted in the Basilica of Notre-Dame d'Alençon. Therese was barely 4<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1020198016">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac" role="math"><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span> years old. Her mother's death dealt her a severe blow and later she would consider that "the first part of her life stopped that day".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2016)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>She wrote: "Every detail of my mother's illness is still with me, specially her last weeks on earth." She remembered the bedroom scene where her dying mother received the last sacraments while Therese knelt and her father cried. She wrote: "When Mummy died, my happy disposition changed. I had been so lively and open; now I became diffident and oversensitive, crying if anyone looked at me. I was only happy if no one took notice of me… It was only in the intimacy of my own family, where everyone was wonderfully kind, that I could be more myself."<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres195966_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres195966-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa1876.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Teresa1876.jpg/180px-Teresa1876.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="197" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6a/Teresa1876.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="219" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa1876.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese (1876)</div></div></div>
<p>Three months after Zélie died, Louis Martin left Alençon, where he had spent his youth and marriage, and moved to <a href="/wiki/Lisieux" title="Lisieux">Lisieux</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Calvados_(department)" title="Calvados (department)">Calvados</a> Department of <a href="/wiki/Normandy" title="Normandy">Normandy</a>, where Zélie's pharmacist brother, Isidore Guérin lived with his wife and their two daughters, Jeanne and Marie. In her last months Zélie had given up the lace business. After her death, Louis sold it. Louis leased a pretty, spacious country house, <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span>, situated in a large garden on the slope of a hill overlooking the town. Looking back, Therese would see the move to <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span> as the beginning of the "second period of my life, the most painful of the three: it extends from the age of four-and-a-half to fourteen, the time when I rediscovered my childhood character, and entered into the serious side of life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (April 2018)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In Lisieux, Pauline took on the role of Therese's "Mama". She took this role seriously, and Therese grew especially close to her, and to Céline, the sister closest to her in age.<sup id="cite_ref-Society_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Society-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years">Early years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lisieux,_Les_Buissonnets.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG/220px-Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG/330px-Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG/440px-Lisieux%2C_Les_Buissonnets.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lisieux,_Les_Buissonnets.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span>, the Martin family house in Lisieux to which they moved in November 1877 following the death of Madame Martin. Therese lived here from 16 November 1877 to 9 April 1888, the day she entered Carmel.</div></div></div>
<p>Therese was taught at home until she was eight and a half, and then entered the school kept by the Benedictine nuns of the Abbey of Notre Dame du Pre in Lisieux. Therese, taught well and carefully by Marie and Pauline, found herself at the top of the class, except for writing and arithmetic. However, because of her young age and high grades, she was bullied. The one who bullied her the most was a girl of fourteen who did poorly at school. Therese suffered very much as a result of her sensitivity, and she cried in silence. Furthermore, the boisterous games at recreation were not to her taste. She preferred to tell stories or look after the little ones in the infants class. "The five years I spent at school were the saddest of my life, and if my dear Céline had not been with me I could not have stayed there for a single month without falling ill." Céline informs us, "She now developed a fondness for hiding,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> she did not want to be observed, for she sincerely considered herself inferior".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres195973_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres195973-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> On her free days she became more and more attached to Marie Guérin, the younger of her two cousins in Lisieux. The two girls would play at being <a href="/wiki/Anchorite" title="Anchorite">anchorites</a>, as <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_Avila" class="mw-redirect" title="Teresa of Avila">the great Teresa</a> had once played with her brother. And every evening she plunged into the family circle. "Fortunately I could go home every evening and then I cheered up. I used to jump on Father's knee and tell him what marks I had, and when he kissed me all my troubles were forgotten… I needed this sort of encouragement so much."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Yet the tension of the double life and the daily self-conquest placed a strain on Therese. Going to school became more and more difficult.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:TdL-1881.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TdL-1881.JPG/180px-TdL-1881.JPG" decoding="async" width="180" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TdL-1881.JPG/270px-TdL-1881.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TdL-1881.JPG/360px-TdL-1881.JPG 2x" data-file-width="432" data-file-height="594" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:TdL-1881.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese aged 8, 1881</div></div></div>
<p>When she was nine years old, in October 1882, her sister Pauline, who had acted as a "second mother"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> to her, entered the Carmelite convent at Lisieux. Therese was devastated. She understood that Pauline was cloistered and that she would never come back. "I said in the depths of my heart: Pauline is lost to me!"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The shock reawakened in her the trauma caused by her mother's death. She also wanted to join the Carmelites, but was told she was too young. Yet Therese so impressed Mother Marie Gonzague, the <a href="/wiki/Prioress" class="mw-redirect" title="Prioress">prioress</a> at the time of Pauline's entry to the community that she wrote to comfort her, calling Therese "my future little daughter".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2016)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Illness">Illness</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Illness">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At this time, Therese was often sick. She began to suffer from nervous tremors. The tremors started one night after her uncle took her for a walk and began to talk about Zélie. Assuming that she was cold, the family covered Therese with blankets, but the tremors continued. She clenched her teeth and could not speak. The family called Dr. Notta, who could make no diagnosis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198319_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198319-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In 1882, Dr. Gayral diagnosed that Therese "reacts to an emotional frustration with a neurotic attack".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199653_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199653-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alarmed, but cloistered, Pauline began to write letters to Therese and attempted various strategies to intervene. Eventually Therese recovered after she had turned to gaze at the statue of the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Mary">Virgin Mary</a> placed in Marie's room, where Therese had been moved.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher199347_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher199347-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> She reported on 13 May 1883 that she had seen the Virgin smile at her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaForest200015_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaForest200015-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> She wrote: "Our Blessed Lady has come to me, she has smiled upon me. How happy I am."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)200632_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)200632-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> However, when Therese told the Carmelite nuns about this vision at the request of her eldest sister Marie, she found herself assailed by their questions and she lost confidence. Self-doubt made her begin to question what had happened. "I thought I <i>had lied</i> – I was unable to look upon myself without a feeling of <i>profound horror</i>."<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> "For a long time after my cure, I thought that my sickness was deliberate and this was a real martyrdom for my soul".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199652_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont199652-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Her concerns over this continued until November 1887.
</p><p>In October 1886, her oldest sister, Marie, entered the same Carmelite monastery, adding to Therese's grief. The warm atmosphere at <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span>, so necessary to her, was disappearing. Now only she and Céline remained with their father. Her frequent tears made some friends think she had a weak character and the Guérins indeed shared this opinion.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Therese also suffered from <a href="/wiki/Scrupulosity" title="Scrupulosity">scruples</a>, a condition experienced by other saints such as <a href="/wiki/Alphonsus_Liguori" title="Alphonsus Liguori">Alphonsus Liguori</a>, also a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_Loyola" class="mw-redirect" title="Ignatius Loyola">Ignatius Loyola</a>, the founder of the <a href="/wiki/Jesuits" title="Jesuits">Jesuits</a>. She wrote: "One would have to pass through this martyrdom to understand it well, and for me to express what I experienced for a year and a half would be impossible".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMonahan200345_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonahan200345-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Complete_conversion">Complete conversion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Complete conversion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa13anni.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Teresa13anni.JPG/180px-Teresa13anni.JPG" decoding="async" width="180" height="239" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Teresa13anni.JPG/270px-Teresa13anni.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/ef/Teresa13anni.JPG 2x" data-file-width="328" data-file-height="436" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa13anni.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese in 1886, age 13</div></div></div>
<p>Christmas Eve of 1886 was a turning point in the life of Therese; she called it her "complete conversion." Years later she stated that on that night she overcame the pressures she had faced since the death of her mother and said that "God worked a little miracle to make me grow up in an instant […] On that blessed night… Jesus, who saw fit to make Himself a child out of love for me, saw fit to have me come forth from the swaddling clothes and imperfections of childhood".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMonahan200354_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonahan200354-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That night, Louis Martin and his daughters, Léonie, Céline and Thérèse, attended <a href="/wiki/Midnight_Mass" title="Midnight Mass">Midnight Mass</a> at the cathedral in Lisieux – "but there was very little heart left in them. On 1 December, Léonie, covered in <a href="/wiki/Eczema" class="mw-redirect" title="Eczema">eczema</a> and hiding her hair under a short <a href="/wiki/Mantilla" title="Mantilla">mantilla</a>, had returned to <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span> after just seven weeks of the <a href="/wiki/Poor_Clares" title="Poor Clares">Poor Clares</a> regime in Alençon", and her sisters were helping her get over her sense of failure and humiliation. Back at <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span> as every year, Thérèse "as was the custom for French children, had left her shoes on the hearth, empty in anticipation of gifts, not from Father Christmas but from the Child Jesus, who was imagined to travel through the air bearing toys and cakes."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200321_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200321-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> While she and Céline were going up the stairs she heard her father, "perhaps exhausted by the hour, or this reminder of the relentless emotional demands of his weepy youngest daughter",<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> say with some irritation "Therese is far too old for this now. Fortunately this will be the last year!"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Therese had begun to cry and Céline advised her not to go back downstairs immediately. Then, suddenly, Therese pulled herself together and wiped her tears. She ran down the stairs, knelt by the fireplace and unwrapped her surprises as jubilantly as ever. In her account, nine years later, of 1895: "In an instant Jesus, content with my good will, accomplished the work I had not been able to do in ten years."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> After nine sad years she had "recovered the strength of soul she had lost"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> when her mother died and, she said, "she was to retain it forever".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> She discovered the joy in self-forgetfulness and added, "I felt, in a word, charity enter my heart, the need to forget myself to make others happy&#160;&#8211;&#32;Since this blessed night I was not defeated in any battle, but instead I went from victory to victory and began, so to speak, 'to run a giant's course'."<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>According to Ida Görres, "Therese instantly understood what had happened to her when she won this banal little <i>victory</i> over her sensitivity, which she had borne for so long; …freedom is found in resolutely looking away from oneself... and the fact that a person can cast himself away from himself reveals again that being good, <i>victory</i> is pure grace, a sudden gift... It cannot be coerced, and yet it can be received only by the patiently prepared heart".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959112_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959112-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Biographer <a href="/wiki/Kathryn_Harrison" title="Kathryn Harrison">Kathryn Harrison</a>: "After all, in the past she <em>had</em> tried to control herself, had tried with all her being and had failed. Grace, alchemy, <a href="/wiki/Self-defeating_personality_disorder" title="Self-defeating personality disorder">masochism</a>: through whatever lens we view her transport, Therese's night of illumination presented both its power and its danger. It would guide her steps between the mortal and the divine, between living and dying, destruction and apotheosis. It would take her exactly where she intended to go".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200363_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200363-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese's character and the early experiences that shaped her have been the subject of analysis, particularly in recent years. Apart from the family doctor who observed her in the 19th century, all other conclusions are inevitably speculative. For instance, author <a href="/wiki/Ida_Friederike_G%C3%B6rres" title="Ida Friederike Görres">Ida Görres</a>, whose formal studies had focused on church history and <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a>, wrote a psychological analysis of the Therese's character. Some authors suggest that Therese had a strongly neurotic aspect to her personality for most of her life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres195983_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres195983-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Harrison concluded that, "her temperament was not formed for compromise or moderation … a life spent not taming but directing her appetite and her will, a life perhaps shortened by the force of her desire and ambition."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200321_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200321-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rome_and_entry_to_Carmel">Rome and entry to Carmel</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Rome and entry to Carmel">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif/220px-Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif/330px-Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif/440px-Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif 2x" data-file-width="471" data-file-height="332" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis_-_De_Imitatione_Christi.gif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>15th-century manuscript of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Imitation_of_Christ" title="The Imitation of Christ">The Imitation of Christ</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Royal_Library_of_Belgium" title="Royal Library of Belgium">Royal Library of Belgium</a></div></div></div>
<p>Before she was fourteen, when she started to experience a period of calm, Therese started to read <i><a href="/wiki/The_Imitation_of_Christ" title="The Imitation of Christ">The Imitation of Christ</a></i>. She read the <i>Imitation</i> intently, as if the author traced each sentence for her: "The Kingdom of God is within you… Turn thee with thy whole heart unto the Lord; and forsake this wretched world: and thy soul shall find rest."<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> She kept the book with her constantly and wrote later that this book and parts of another book of a very different character, lectures by Abbé Charles Arminjon on <i>The End of This World, and the Mysteries of the World to Come</i>, nourished her during this critical period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959126–127_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959126–127-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Thereafter she began to read other books, mostly on history and science.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198322_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198322-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1887, Therese approached her 63-year-old father Louis, who was recovering from a small stroke, while he sat in the garden one Sunday afternoon and told him that she wanted to celebrate the anniversary of "her conversion" by entering Carmel before Christmas. Louis and Thérèse both broke down and cried, but Louis got up, gently picked a little white flower, root intact, and gave it to her, explaining the care with which God brought it into being and preserved it until that day. Thérèse later wrote: "while I listened I believed I was hearing my own story".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> To Therese, the flower seemed a symbol of herself, "destined to live in another soil".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Therese renewed her attempts to join the Carmel, but the priest-superior of the monastery would not allow it on account of her youth.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2640" data-file-height="1914" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pranzini_photo_anthropom%C3%A9trique_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>1887 Police Mugshot of Henri Pranzini</div></div></div>
<p>During the summer, French newspapers were filled with the story of <a href="/w/index.php?title=Henri_Pranzini&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Henri Pranzini (page does not exist)">Henri Pranzini</a>, convicted of the brutal murder of two women and a child. To the outraged public Pranzini represented all that threatened the decent way of life in France. In July and August 1887 Therese prayed hard for the conversion of Pranzini, so his soul could be saved, yet Pranzini showed no remorse. At the end of August, the newspapers reported that just as Pranzini's neck was placed on the guillotine, he had grabbed a <a href="/wiki/Crucifix" title="Crucifix">crucifix</a> and kissed it three times. Therese was ecstatic and believed that her prayers had saved him. She continued to pray for Pranzini after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198334_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor198334-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:168px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Therese.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8d/Therese.jpg" decoding="async" width="166" height="300" class="thumbimage" data-file-width="166" data-file-height="300" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Therese.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese at age 15. Before seeking permission to enter Carmel on Christmas 1887, she had put up her hair for the first time, a symbol of passing childhood. "A photograph taken in April 1888 shows a fresh, firm, girlish face<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span> […] The familiar flowing locks are combed sternly back and up, piled in a hard little <a href="/wiki/Chignon_(hairstyle)" title="Chignon (hairstyle)">chignon</a> on the top of her head".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959149_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959149-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Leo_XIII,_1887.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg/180px-Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="244" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg/270px-Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg/360px-Leo_XIII%2C_1887.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5891" data-file-height="8000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Leo_XIII,_1887.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Leo_XIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Leo XIII">Leo XIII</a> – in November 1887 when Therese met him, he was seventy-seven.</div></div></div>
<p>In November 1887, Louis took Céline and Therese on a diocesan pilgrimage to Rome for the priestly jubilee of Pope <a href="/wiki/Leo_XIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Leo XIII">Leo&#160;XIII</a>. On 20 November 1887, during a general <a href="/wiki/Audience_(head_of_state)" class="mw-redirect" title="Audience (head of state)">audience</a> with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIII" title="Pope Leo XIII">Leo XIII</a>, Therese, in her turn, approached the Pope, knelt, and asked him to allow her to enter Carmel at 15. The Pope said: "Well, my child, do what the superiors decide… You will enter if it is God's Will" and he blessed Therese. She refused to leave his feet, and the Noble Guard had to carry her out of the room.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The trip continued: they visited <a href="/wiki/Pompeii" title="Pompeii">Pompeii</a>, <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a>, <a href="/wiki/Assisi" title="Assisi">Assisi</a> before going back via <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a>. The pilgrimage of nearly a month was timely. During it she "learnt more than in many years of study". For the first and last time in her life, she left her native Normandy. Notably she "who only knew priests in the exercise of their ministry was in their company, heard their conversations, not always edifying&#160;&#8211;&#32;and saw their shortcomings for herself".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher199377_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher199377-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She had understood that she had to pray and give her life for sinners like Pranzini. But Carmel prayed especially for priests and this had surprised her since their souls seemed to her to be "as pure as crystal". A month spent with many priests taught her that they are "weak and feeble men". She wrote later: "I met many saintly priests that month, but I also found that in spite of being above angels by their supreme dignity, they were none the less men and still subject to human weakness. If the holy priests, 'the salt of the earth', as Jesus calls them in the Gospel, have to be prayed for, what about the lukewarm? Again, as Jesus says, 'If the salt shall lose its savour, wherewith shall it be salted?' I understood my vocation in Italy." For the first time too she had associated with young men. "In her brotherless existence, masculinity had been represented only by her father, her Uncle Guérin and various priests. Now she had her first and only experiences. Céline declared at the beatification proceedings that one of the young men in the pilgrimage group "developed a tender affection for her". Thérèse confessed to her sister, "It is high time for Jesus to remove me from the poisonous breath of the world […] I feel that my heart is easily caught by tenderness, and where others fall, I would fall too. We are no stronger than the others".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959153–154_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959153–154-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Soon after that, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Bayeux" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Bayeux">Bishop of Bayeux</a> authorized the prioress to receive Thérèse. On 9 April 1888 she became a Carmelite <a href="/wiki/Postulant" title="Postulant">postulant</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Lisieux_Carmel">Lisieux Carmel</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Lisieux Carmel">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Carmel_Lisieux.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Carmel_Lisieux.jpg/220px-Carmel_Lisieux.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Carmel_Lisieux.jpg/330px-Carmel_Lisieux.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Carmel_Lisieux.jpg/440px-Carmel_Lisieux.jpg 2x" data-file-width="768" data-file-height="504" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Carmel_Lisieux.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Lisieux convent</div></div></div><p> The convent Thérèse entered was an old-established house with a long tradition. In 1838 two nuns from the <a href="/wiki/Poitiers" title="Poitiers">Poitiers</a> Carmel had been sent out to found the house of Lisieux. One of them, Mother Geneviève of St Teresa, was still living. When Thérèse entered the second wing, containing the cells and sickrooms in which she was to live and die, which had been standing only ten years, "What she found was a community of very aged nuns, some odd and cranky, some sick and troubled, some lukewarm and complacent. Almost all of the sisters came from the petty bourgeois and artisan class. The Prioress and Novice Mistress were of old <a href="/wiki/Normandy" title="Normandy">Normandy</a> nobility. Probably the Martin sisters alone represented the new class of the rising bourgeoisie".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959193–195_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959193–195-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Carmelite order had been reformed in the sixteenth century by <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>, essentially devoted to personal and collective prayer. The nuns of Lisieux followed a strict regimen that allowed for only one meal a day for seven months of the year, and little free time. Only one room of the building was heated. The times of silence and of solitude were many but the foundress had also planned for time for work and relaxation in common&#160;&#8211;&#32;the austerity of the life should not hinder sisterly and joyful relations. Founded in 1838, the Carmel of Lisieux in 1888 had 26 religious, from very different classes and backgrounds. For the majority of the life of Therese, the prioress would be Mother Marie de Gonzague, born Marie-Adéle-Rosalie Davy de Virville. When Therese entered the convent Mother Marie was 54, a woman of changeable humour, jealous of her authority, used sometimes in a capricious manner; this had for effect, a certain laxity in the observance of established rules. "In the sixties and seventies of the [nineteenth] century an aristocrat in the flesh counted for far more in a petty bourgeois convent than we can realize nowadays […] the superiors appointed Marie de Gonzague to the highest offices as soon as her noviciate was finished […] in 1874 began the long series of terms as Prioress".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959201_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959201-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Postulancy">Postulancy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Postulancy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px .ambox-delete{border-left:10px solid #b32424}.mw-parser-output .ambox-content{border-left:10px solid #f28500}.mw-parser-output .ambox-style{border-left:10px solid #fc3}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2016</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Therese's time as a postulant began with her welcome into the Carmel, Monday, 9 April 1888.<sup id="cite_ref-O_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> She felt peace after she received communion that day and later wrote, "At last my desires were realized, and I cannot describe the deep sweet peace which filled my soul. This peace has remained with me during the eight and a half years of my life here, and has never left me even amid the greatest trials".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)200663_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)200663-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From her childhood, Therese had dreamed of the desert to which God would some day lead her. Now she had entered that desert. Though she was now reunited with Marie and Pauline, from the first day she began her struggle to win and keep her distance from her sisters. Right at the start Marie de Gonzague, the prioress, had turned the postulant Therese over to her eldest sister Marie, who was to teach her to follow the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Divine Office</a>. Later she appointed Therese assistant to Pauline in the refectory. When her cousin Marie Guerin also entered, she employed the two together in the <a href="/wiki/Sacristy" title="Sacristy">sacristy</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Therese adhered strictly to the rule which forbade all superfluous talk during work. She saw her sisters together only in the hours of common recreation after meals. At such times she would sit down beside whomever she happened to be near, or beside a nun whom she had observed to be downcast, disregarding the tacit and sometimes expressed sensitivity and even jealousy of her biological sisters. "We must apologize to the others for our being four under one roof",<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> she was in the habit of remarking. "When I am dead, you must be very careful not to lead a family life with one another […] I did not come to Carmel to be with my sisters; on the contrary, I saw clearly that their presence would cost me dear, for I was determined not to give way to nature."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2016)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Although the novice mistress, Sister Marie of the Angels, found Therese slow, the young postulant adapted well to her new environment. She wrote, "Illusions, the Good Lord gave me the grace to have none on entering Carmel. I found religious life as I had figured, no sacrifice astonished me."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (Octobre 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>She chose a spiritual director, a <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a>, Father Pichon. At their first meeting, 28 May 1888, she made a general confession going back over all her past sins. She came away from it profoundly relieved. The priest who had himself suffered from <a href="/wiki/Scrupulosity" title="Scrupulosity">scruples</a>, understood her and reassured her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher199392_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher199392-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> A few months later, he left for Canada, and Therese would only be able to ask his advice by letter and his replies were rare. (On 4 July 1897, she confided to Pauline, "Father Pichon treated me too much like a child; nonetheless he did me a lot of good too by saying that I never committed a mortal sin."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>) During her time as a postulant, Therese had to endure some bullying from other sisters because of her lack of aptitude for handicrafts and manual work. Sister St Vincent de Paul, the finest embroiderer in the community made her feel awkward and even called her 'the big nanny goat'. Therese was in fact the tallest in the family at 1.62&#160;m (5&#160;ft 4&#160;in). Pauline, the shortest, was no more than <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1020198016"/>1.54&#160;m (5&#160;ft <span class="frac" role="math"><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span>&#160;in) tall.
</p><p>Like all religious she discovered the ups and downs related to differences in temperament, character, problems of sensitivities or infirmities. After nine years she wrote plainly, "the lack of judgment, education, the touchiness of some characters, all these things do not make life very pleasant. I know very well that these moral weaknesses are chronic, that there is no hope of cure".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> But the greatest suffering came from outside Carmel. On 23 June 1888, Louis Martin disappeared from his home and was found days later, in the post office in <a href="/wiki/Le_Havre" title="Le Havre">Le Havre</a>. The incident marked the onset of her father's decline. He died on July 29, 1894.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Novitiate">Novitiate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Novitiate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Therese_von_Lisieux.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Therese_von_Lisieux.jpg/180px-Therese_von_Lisieux.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="217" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c2/Therese_von_Lisieux.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="247" data-file-height="298" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Therese_von_Lisieux.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese of Lisieux, photograph, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1888–1896</span></div></div></div>
<p>The end of Therese's time as a postulant arrived on the January 10, 1889, with her taking of the habit. From that time she wore the "rough homespun and brown <a href="/wiki/Scapular" title="Scapular">scapular</a>, white <a href="/wiki/Wimple" title="Wimple">wimple</a> and veil, leather belt with <a href="/wiki/Rosary" title="Rosary">rosary</a>, woollen 'stockings', rope sandals".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher199399_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher199399-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Her father's health having temporarily stabilized he was able to attend, though twelve days after her ceremony her father suffered a stroke and was taken to a private sanatorium, the Bon Sauveur at <a href="/wiki/Caen" title="Caen">Caen</a>, where he remained for three years before returning to Lisieux in 1892. In this period Therese deepened the sense of her vocation; to lead a hidden life, to pray and offer her suffering for priests, to forget herself, to increase discreet acts of charity. She wrote, "I applied myself especially to practice little virtues, not having the facility to perform great ones […] In her letters from this period of her novitiate, Therese returned over and over to the theme of littleness, referring to herself as a grain of sand, an image she borrowed from Pauline… 'Always littler, lighter, in order to be lifted more easily by the breeze of love'.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The remainder of her life would be defined by retreat and subtraction".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200391_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200391-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She absorbed the work of <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a>, spiritual reading uncommon at the time, especially for such a young nun. "Oh! what insights I have gained from the works of our holy father, St. John of the Cross! When I was seventeen and eighteen, I had no other spiritual nourishment…" She felt a kinship with this classic writer of the Carmelite Order (though nothing seems to have drawn her to the writing of <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_Avila" class="mw-redirect" title="Teresa of Avila">Teresa of Avila</a>), and with enthusiasm she read his works, <i>The <a href="/wiki/Ascent_of_Mount_Carmel" title="Ascent of Mount Carmel">Ascent of Mount Carmel</a></i>, the <i>Way of Purification</i>, the <i>Spiritual Canticle</i>, the <i>Living Flame of Love</i>. Passages from these writings are woven into everything she herself said and wrote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959250–251_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959250–251-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The fear of God, which she found in certain sisters, paralyzed her. "My nature is such that fear makes me recoil, with LOVE not only do I go forward, I fly".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993109_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993109-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the new name a Carmelite receives when she enters the Order, there is always an epithet – for example, Teresa of Jesus, <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_of_the_Trinity" title="Elizabeth of the Trinity">Elizabeth of the Trinity</a>, Anne of the Angels. The epithet singles out the Mystery which she is supposed to contemplate with special devotion. "Therese's names in religion – she had two – must be taken together to define their religious significance".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959258_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959258-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The first name was promised to her at nine, by Mother Marie de Gonzague, <i>of the Child Jesus</i>, and was given to her on her entry to the convent. In itself, veneration of the childhood of Jesus was a Carmelite heritage of the seventeenth century – it concentrated upon the staggering humiliation of divine majesty in assuming the shape of extreme weakness and helplessness. The French <a href="/wiki/Oratory_of_Jesus" title="Oratory of Jesus">Oratory of Jesus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pierre_de_B%C3%A9rulle" title="Pierre de Bérulle">Pierre de Bérulle</a> renewed this old devotional practice. Yet when she received the veil, Therese herself asked Mother Marie de Gonzague to confer upon her the second name <i>of the Holy Face</i>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"/><table class="sidebar nomobile nowraplinks"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/File:Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg" class="image"><img alt="Emblem of the Papacy SE.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/25px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png" decoding="async" width="25" height="34" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/38px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/50px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="815" data-file-height="1105" /></a><br />Part of <i><a href="/wiki/Category:Visions_of_Jesus_and_Mary" title="Category:Visions of Jesus and Mary">a series</a></i> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/Catholic_devotions" title="Catholic devotions">Catholic devotions</a><br />to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Face_of_Jesus" title="Holy Face of Jesus">Holy Face of Jesus</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Turin_plasch.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Turin plasch.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Turin_plasch.jpg/100px-Turin_plasch.jpg" decoding="async" width="100" height="129" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/27/Turin_plasch.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="150" data-file-height="194" /></a></td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">
Prayers and <a href="/wiki/Sacramental" title="Sacramental">sacramentals</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">

</tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">
People</th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">

<li><a class="mw-selflink selflink">Thérèse of Lisieux</a></li>
