</tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">
Organisations</th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Sisters_of_the_Reparation_of_the_Holy_Face" title="Sisters of the Reparation of the Holy Face">Sisters of the Reparation of the Holy Face</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sisters_of_the_Holy_Face" title="Sisters of the Holy Face">Sisters of the Holy Face</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Oratory_of_the_Holy_Face" title="Oratory of the Holy Face">Oratory of the Holy Face</a></li></ul></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="noviewer thumbborder" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Holy_Face_of_Jesus" title="Template:Holy Face of Jesus"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Holy_Face_of_Jesus" title="Template talk:Holy Face of Jesus"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Holy_Face_of_Jesus&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>During the course of her novitiate, contemplation of the Holy Face was said to have nourished her inner life. This is an image representing the disfigured face of Jesus during the Passion. She meditated on certain passages from the prophet <a href="/wiki/Isaiah" title="Isaiah">Isaiah</a> (Chapter 53). Six weeks before her death she remarked to Pauline, "The words in Isaiah: 'no stateliness here, no majesty, no beauty, […] one despised, left out of all human reckoning; How should we take any account of him, a man so despised (<abbr title="Isaiah">Is</abbr> 53:2–3)<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> – these words were the basis of my whole worship of the Holy Face. I, too, wanted to be without comeliness and beauty, unknown to all creatures."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)1977&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMYJvej6xMU8CpgPA135_135&#93;&quot;The_Yellow_Notebook&quot;_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)1977[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMYJvej6xMU8CpgPA135_135]&quot;The_Yellow_Notebook&quot;-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> On the eve of her profession she wrote to Sister Marie, "Tomorrow I shall be the bride of Jesus 'whose face was hidden and whom no man knew' – what a union and what a future!".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959261_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959261-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The meditation also helped her understand the humiliating situation of her father.
</p><p>Usually the novitiate preceding profession lasted a year. Sister Therese hoped to make her final commitment on or after 11 January 1890 but, considered still too young for a final commitment, her profession was postponed. She would spend eight months longer than the standard year as an unprofessed novice. As 1889 ended, her old home in the world, <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Les Buissonnets</i></span>, was dismantled, the furniture divided among the Guérins and the Carmel. It was not until 8 September 1890, aged 17 and a half, that she made her religious profession. The retreat in anticipation of her "irrevocable promises" was characterized by "absolute aridity" and on the eve of her profession she gave way to panic. She worried that "What she wanted was beyond her. Her vocation was a sham".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200397_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200397-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Reassured by the novice mistress and mother Marie de Gonzague, the next day her religious profession went ahead, "an outpouring of peace flooded my soul, "that peace which surpasseth all understanding" (<a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Philippians" title="Epistle to the Philippians">Phil.</a> 4:7)".<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Against her heart she wore her letter of profession written during her retreat. "May creatures be nothing for me, and may I be nothing for them, but may You, Jesus, be everything! Let nobody be occupied with me, let me be looked upon as one to be trampled underfoot […] may Your will be done in me perfectly… Jesus, allow me to save very many souls; let no soul be lost today; let all the souls in purgatory be saved…" On September 24, the public ceremony followed filled with 'sadness and bitterness'. "Therese found herself young enough, alone enough, to weep over the absence of Bishop Hugonin, Père Pichon, in Canada; and her own father, still confined in the asylum".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200398_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200398-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> But Mother Marie de Gonzague wrote to the prioress of Tours, "The angelic child is seventeen and a half, and she has the judgment of one of thirty, the religious perfection of an old perfected novice, and possession of herself; she is a perfect religious."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison200363_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200363-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Life_as_a_Carmelite">Life as a Carmelite</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Life as a Carmelite">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The years which followed were a maturation. Therese prayed without great sensitive emotions, she increased the small acts of charity and care for others, doing small services. She accepted criticism in silence, even unjust criticisms, and smiled at the sisters who were unpleasant to her. She always prayed for priests, and in particular for Father <a href="/wiki/Hyacinthe_Loyson" title="Hyacinthe Loyson">Hyacinthe Loyson</a>, a famous preacher who had been a <a href="/wiki/Sulpician" class="mw-redirect" title="Sulpician">Sulpician</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican</a> novice before becoming a Carmelite and provincial of his order, but who had left the Catholic Church in 1869. Three years later he married a young Protestant widow, with whom he had a son. After his excommunication, he continued to travel around France giving lectures. While clerical papers called Loyson a "renegade monk" and <a href="/wiki/Leon_Bloy" class="mw-redirect" title="Leon Bloy">Leon Bloy</a> lampooned him, Therese prayed for her "brother". She offered her last communion, 19 August 1897, for Father Loyson.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The chaplain to the Carmel, Father Youf, insisted a lot on the fear of Hell. The preachers during spiritual retreats at that time emphasised sin, the sufferings of <a href="/wiki/Purgatory" title="Purgatory">purgatory</a>, and those of hell. This did not help Therese who in 1891 experienced, "great inner trials of all kinds, even wondering sometimes whether heaven existed." One phrase heard during a sermon made her weep: "No one knows if they are worthy of love or of hate." However the retreat of October 1891 was preached by Father Alexis Prou, a <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> from <a href="/wiki/Saint-Nazaire" title="Saint-Nazaire">Saint-Nazaire</a>. "He specialized in large crowds (he preached in factories) and did not seem the right person to help Carmelites. Just one of them found comfort in his words, Sister Thèrèse of the Child Jesus […] [his] preaching on abandonment and mercy expanded her heart".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993118_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher1993118-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This confirmed her own intuitions. She wrote, "My soul was like a book which the priest read better than I did. He launched me full sail on the waves of confidence and love which held such an attraction for me, but upon which I had not dared to venture. He told me that my faults did not offend God." Her spiritual life drew more and more on the <a href="/wiki/Gospels" class="mw-redirect" title="Gospels">Gospels</a> that she carried with her at all times. The piety of her time was fed more on commentaries, but Therese had asked Céline to get the Gospels and the <a href="/wiki/Epistles_of_St_Paul" class="mw-redirect" title="Epistles of St Paul">Epistles of St Paul</a> bound into a single small volume which she could carry on her heart. She said, "But it is especially the Gospels which sustain me during my hours of prayer, for in them I find what is necessary for my poor little soul. I am constantly discovering in them new lights, hidden and mysterious meanings."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Over time Therese realised that she felt no attraction to the exalted heights of "great souls". She looked directly for the word of Jesus, which shed light on her prayers and on her daily life. Therese's retreat in October 1892 pointed to a "downward" path for her. If asked where she lived, she would pause and quote, "The foxes have their lairs, the birds of heaven their nests, but I have no place to rest my head." (<a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Matthew</a> 8:20). She wrote to Céline (letter 19 October 1892), "Jesus raised us above all the fragile things of this world whose image passes away. Like <a href="/wiki/Zacchaeus" title="Zacchaeus">Zacchaeus</a>, we climbed a tree to see Jesus and now let us listen to what he is saying to us. Make haste to descend, I must lodge today at your house. Well, Jesus tells us to descend?" "A question here of the interior," she qualified in her letter, lest Céline think she meant renouncing food or shelter. "Therese knew her virtues, even her love, to be flawed, flawed by self, a mirror too clouded to reflect the divine." She continued to seek to discover the means, "more efficiently to strip herself of self".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003108_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003108-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> "No doubt, [our hearts] are already empty of creatures, but, alas, I feel mine is not entirely empty of myself, and it is for this reason that Jesus tells me to descend."<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_Mother_Agnes">Election of Mother Agnes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Election of Mother Agnes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 20 February 1893, Pauline was elected prioress of Carmel and became "Mother Agnes". She appointed the former prioress as novice mistress and made Therese her assistant. The work of guiding the novices would fall primarily to Therese. She repeated how important respect for the Rule was: "When any break the rule, this is not a reason to justify ourselves. Each must act as if the perfection of the Order depended on her personal conduct."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> She also affirmed the essential role of obedience in religious life. She said, "When you stop watching the infallible compass [of obedience], as quickly the mind wanders in arid lands where the water of grace is soon lacking."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2012)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Over the next few years she revealed a talent for clarifying doctrine to those who had not received as much education as she. A kaleidoscope, whose three mirrors transform scraps of coloured paper into beautiful designs, provided an inspired illustration for the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Trinity" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Trinity">Holy Trinity</a>. "As long as our actions, even the smallest, do not fall away from the focus of Divine Love, the Holy Trinity, symbolized by the three mirrors, allows them to reflect wonderful beauty. Jesus, who regards us through the little lens, that is to say, through Himself, always sees beauty in everything we do. But if we left the focus of inexpressible love, what would He see? Bits of straw […] dirty, worthless actions".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959114_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959114-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> "Another cherished image was that of the newly invented elevator, a vehicle Therese used many times over to describe God's grace, a force that lifts us to heights we can't reach on our own".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003111_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003111-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Martha of Jesus, a novice who spent her childhood in a series of orphanages and who was described by all as emotionally unbalanced, with a violent temper, gave witness during the beatification process of the 'unusual dedication and presence of her young teacher. "Therese deliberately 'sought out the company of those nuns whose temperaments she found hardest to bear.' What merit was there in acting charitably toward people whom one loved naturally? Therese went out of her way to spend time with, and therefore to love, the people she found repellent. It was an effective means of achieving interior poverty, a way to remove a place to rest her head".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003111_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003111-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1893, Therese, having been a temporarily professed for the standard three years, asked not to be promoted but to continue a novice indefinitely. As a novice she would always have to ask permission of the other, full sisters. She would never be elected to any position of importance. Remaining closely associated with the other novices, she could continue to care for her spiritual charges. In 1841 <a href="/wiki/Jules_Michelet" title="Jules Michelet">Jules Michelet</a> devoted the major part of the fifth volume of his <i>History of France</i> to a favourable presentation of the epic of <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_Arc" title="Joan of Arc">Joan of Arc</a>. <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Dupanloup" title="Félix Dupanloup">Félix Dupanloup</a> worked relentlessly for the glorification of Joan who, on 8 May 1429 had liberated <a href="/wiki/Orl%C3%A9ans" title="Orléans">Orléans</a>, the city of which he became bishop in 1849. Therese wrote two plays in honour of her childhood heroine, the first about Joan's response to the heavenly voices calling her to battle, the second about her resulting <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyrdom</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>1894 brought a national celebration of Joan of Arc. On 27 January, <a href="/wiki/Leo_XIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Leo XIII">Leo XIII</a> authorized the introduction of her cause of beatification, declaring Joan, the shepherdess from <a href="/wiki/Lorraine_(province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorraine (province)">Lorraine</a> "venerable". Thérèse used <a href="/wiki/Henri-Alexandre_Wallon" title="Henri-Alexandre Wallon">Henri Wallon</a>'s history of Joan of Arc – a book her uncle Isidore had given to the Carmel – to help her write two plays, "pious recreations", "small theatrical pieces performed by a few nuns for the rest of the community, on the occasion of certain feast days". The first of these, <i>The Mission of Joan of Arc</i>, was performed at the Carmel on 21 January 1894, and the second, <i>Joan of Arc Accomplishes her Mission</i>, exactly one year later, on 21 January 1895. In the estimation of one of her biographers, Ida Görres, they "are scarcely veiled self-portraits".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959401_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959401-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> On 29 July 1894, Louis Martin died.
</p>
<h3><span id="The_.22little_way.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_&quot;little_way&quot;">The "little way"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: The &quot;little way&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Therese entered the Carmel of Lisieux with the determination to become a saint. However, by the end of 1894, six years as a Carmelite made her realize how small and insignificant she felt. She saw the limitations of all her efforts. She remained small and very far off from the unfailing love that she would wish to practice. She is said to have understood then that it was from insignificance that she had to learn to ask God's help. Along with her camera, Céline had brought notebooks with her, passages from the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a>, which Therese did not have in Carmel. (The Louvain Bible, the translation authorized for French Catholics, did not include the Old Testament.) In the notebooks Therese found a passage from <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Proverbs" title="Book of Proverbs">Proverbs</a> that struck her with particular force: "Whosoever is a little one, let him come to me" (Proverbs 9:4).<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She was struck by another passage from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Isaiah" title="Book of Isaiah">Book of Isaiah</a>: "you shall be carried at the breasts, and upon the knees they shall caress you. As one whom the mother caresseth, so will I comfort you." (Isaiah 66:12–13)<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> She concluded that Jesus would carry her to the summit of sanctity. The smallness of Therese, her limits, became in this way grounds for joy, rather than discouragement. Not until Manuscript C of her autobiography did she give this discovery the name of <i>little way</i>, <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">"petite voie"</i></span><sup id="cite_ref-pv_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pv-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I will seek out a means of getting to Heaven by a little way&#160;&#8211;&#32;very short and very straight little way that is wholly new. We live in an age of inventions; nowadays the rich need not trouble to climb the stairs, they have <a href="/wiki/Elevator" title="Elevator">lifts</a> instead. Well, I mean to try and find a lift by which I may be raised unto God, for I am too tiny to climb the steep stairway of perfection. […] Thine Arms, then, O Jesus, are the lift which must raise me up even unto Heaven. To get there I need not grow. On the contrary, I must remain little, I must become still less<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In her quest for sanctity and in order to attain holiness and to express her <a href="/wiki/Love_of_God" title="Love of God">love of God</a>, she believed that it was not necessary to accomplish heroic acts or <i>great deeds</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-GA_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GA-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> She wrote, "Love proves itself by deeds, so how am I to show my love? Great deeds are forbidden me. The only way I can prove my love is by scattering flowers and these flowers are every little sacrifice, every glance and word, and the doing of the least actions for love."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><i>The little way</i> of Therese is the foundation of her spirituality.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Within the Catholic Church Thérèse's way was known for some time as "the little way of spiritual childhood,"<sup id="cite_ref-The_Life_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Life-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-GA_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GA-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> but Therese actually wrote "little way" only three times,<sup id="cite_ref-pv_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pv-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> and she never wrote the phrase "spiritual childhood." It was her sister Pauline who, after Therese's death, adopted the phrase "the little way of spiritual childhood" to interpret Therese's path.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Years after Therese's death, a Carmelite of Lisieux asked Pauline about this phrase and Pauline answered spontaneously "But you know well that Therese never used it! It is mine."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In May 1897, Therese wrote to Father Adolphe Roulland, "My way is all confidence and love."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> To Maurice Bellière she wrote, "and I, with <i>my way</i>, will do more than you, so I hope that one day Jesus will make you walk by the same way as me."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Sometimes, when I read spiritual treatises in which perfection is shown with a thousand obstacles, surrounded by a crowd of illusions, my poor little mind quickly tires. I close the learned book which is breaking my head and drying up my heart, and I take up Holy Scripture. Then all seems luminous to me; a single word uncovers for my soul infinite horizons; perfection seems simple; I see that it is enough to recognize one's nothingness and to abandon oneself, like a child, into God's arms. Leaving to great souls, to great minds, the beautiful books I cannot understand, I rejoice to be little because only children, and those who are like them, will be admitted to the heavenly banquet.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (December 2019)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Merciful_love"><i>Merciful love</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Merciful love">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At the end of the second play that Therese had written on <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_Arc" title="Joan of Arc">Joan of Arc</a>, the costume she wore almost caught fire. The alcohol stoves used to represent the stake at <a href="/wiki/Rouen" title="Rouen">Rouen</a> set fire to the screen behind which Therese stood. Therese did not flinch but the incident marked her. The theme of fire would assume an increasing importance in her writings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996219_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996219-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> On 9 June 1895, during a Mass celebrating the feast of the Holy Trinity, Therese had a sudden inspiration that she must offer herself as a sacrificial victim to <i>merciful love</i>. At this time some nuns offered themselves as a victim to God's <i>justice</i>. In her cell she drew up an 'Act of Oblation' for herself and for Céline, and on 11 June, the two of them knelt before the miraculous Virgin and Therese read the document she had written and signed. "In the evening of this life, I shall appear before You with empty hands, for I do not ask You Lord to count my works."<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to biographer Ida Görres, the document echoed the happiness she had felt when Father Alexis Prou, the Franciscan preacher, had assured her that her faults did not cause God sorrow. In the Oblation she wrote, "If through weakness I should chance to fall, may a glance from Your Eyes straightway cleanse my soul, and consume all my imperfections – as fire transforms all things into itself".<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:AdolpheRoulland.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/AdolpheRoulland.JPG/180px-AdolpheRoulland.JPG" decoding="async" width="180" height="181" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0e/AdolpheRoulland.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="252" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:AdolpheRoulland.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Father Adolphe Roulland of the Society of Foreign Missions</div></div></div>
<p>In August 1895 the four Martin sisters were joined in the convent by their cousin, Marie Guerin, who became Sister Marie of the Eucharist. Léonie, after several attempts, became Sister Françoise-Thérèse, a nun in the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Visitation_of_Holy_Mary" title="Order of the Visitation of Holy Mary">Order of the Visitation of Holy Mary</a> in Caen, where she died in 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At age 14, Therese understood her vocation was to pray for priests, to be "an apostle to apostles". In September 1890, at her canonical examination before she professed her religious vows, she was asked why she had come to Carmel. She answered "I came to save souls, and especially to pray for priests". Throughout her life she prayed fervently for priests, and she corresponded with and prayed for a young priest, Adolphe Roulland, and a young seminarian, Maurice Bellière. She wrote to her sister "Our mission as Carmelites is to form evangelical workers who will save thousands of souls whose mothers we shall be."<sup id="cite_ref-O_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1895 a young seminarian and subdeacon of the <a href="/wiki/White_Fathers" title="White Fathers">White Fathers</a>, Abbé Bellière, asked the Carmel of Lisieux for a nun who would support – by prayer and sacrifice – his missionary work, and the souls that were in the future to be entrusted to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959188_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959188-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Mother Agnes designated Therese. She never met Father Bellière but ten letters passed between them.
</p><p>A year later Father Adolphe Roulland (1870–1934) of the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Foreign_Missions_Society" title="Paris Foreign Missions Society">Society of Foreign Missions</a> requested the same service of the Lisieux Carmel, before entering the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Sichuan" title="Catholic Church in Sichuan">mission field of Sichuan</a>, West China.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Once more Therese was assigned the duties of <i>spiritual sister</i>. "It is quite clear that Therese, in spite of all her reverence for the priestly office, in both cases felt herself to be the teacher and the giver. It is she who consoles and warns, encourages and praises, answers questions, offers corroboration, and instructs the priests in the meaning of her <i>little way</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959189_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959189-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years">Final years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Final years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Therese's final years were marked by a steady decline that she bore resolutely and without complaint. <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">Tuberculosis</a> was the key element of Therese's final suffering, but she saw that as part of her spiritual journey. After observing a rigorous Lenten fast in 1896, she went to bed on the eve of Good Friday and felt a joyous sensation. She wrote: "Oh! how sweet this memory really is! […] I had scarcely laid my head upon the pillow when I felt something like a bubbling stream mounting to my lips. I didn't know what it was." The next morning her handkerchief was soaked in blood and she understood her fate. Coughing up of blood meant tuberculosis, and tuberculosis meant death. She wrote, "I thought immediately of the joyful thing that I had to learn, so I went over to the window. I was able to see that I was not mistaken. Ah! my soul was filled with a great consolation; I was interiorly persuaded that Jesus, on the anniversary of His own death, wanted to have me hear His first call!"<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese corresponded with a Carmelite mission in what was then <a href="/wiki/French_Indochina" title="French Indochina">French Indochina</a> and was invited to join them, but, because of her sickness, could not travel. Tuberculosis slowly devoured her flesh. When she was near death, "Her physical suffering kept increasing so that even the doctor himself was driven to exclaim, "Ah! If you only knew what this young nun was suffering!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson200154_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson200154-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> During the last hours of Therese's life, she said, "I would never have believed it was possible to suffer so much, never, never!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson200162_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson200162-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In July 1897, she made a final move to the monastery infirmary. On August 19, 1897, she received her last communion. She died on 30 September 1897, aged 24. On her deathbed, she is reported to have said, "I have reached the point of not being able to suffer any more, because all suffering is sweet to me." Her last words were, "My God, I love you!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)1996271_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)1996271-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese was buried on 4 October 1897, in the Carmelite plot, in the municipal cemetery at Lisieux, where her parents had been buried. Her body was exhumed in September 1910 and the remains placed in a lead coffin and transferred to another tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-Lagoutte_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lagoutte-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In March 1923, however, before she was beatified, her body was returned to the Carmel of Lisieux, where it remains. The figure of Therese in the glass coffin is not her actual body but a <i><a href="/wiki/Gisant" class="mw-redirect" title="Gisant">gisant</a></i> statue based on drawings and photos by Céline after Therese's death. It contains her ribcage and other remnants of her body.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Spirituality">Spirituality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Spirituality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"/><table class="sidebar nomobile nowraplinks"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-top-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg" class="image"><img alt="Emblem of the Papacy SE.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/25px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png" decoding="async" width="25" height="34" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/38px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg/50px-Emblem_of_the_Papacy_SE.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="815" data-file-height="1105" /></a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle-with-top-image">Part of <a href="/wiki/Category:Eucharist_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Category:Eucharist in the Catholic Church">a series</a> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/Eucharistic_adoration" title="Eucharistic adoration">Eucharistic adoration</a><br />of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Monstrans2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Monstrans2.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Monstrans2.jpg/110px-Monstrans2.jpg" decoding="async" width="110" height="118" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Monstrans2.jpg/165px-Monstrans2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b5/Monstrans2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="178" data-file-height="191" /></a><div class="sidebar-caption">Solar <a href="/wiki/Monstrance" title="Monstrance">monstrance</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a></div></td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="noviewer thumbborder" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eucharistic_adoration" title="Template:Eucharistic adoration"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eucharistic_adoration" title="Template talk:Eucharistic adoration"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eucharistic_adoration&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>To the right and to the left, I throw to my little birds the good grain that God places in my hands. And then I let things take their course! I busy myself with it no more. Sometimes, it's just as though I had thrown nothing; at other times, it does some good. But God tells me: 'Give, give always, without being concerned with the results<span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)1977&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMYJvej6xMU8CpgPA44_44&#93;&quot;The_Yellow_Notebook&quot;_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseTaylor_(tr.)1977[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMYJvej6xMU8CpgPA44_44]&quot;The_Yellow_Notebook&quot;-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Together with <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>, Therese of Lisieux is one of the most popular Roman Catholic saints since apostolic times. She is approachable, due in part to her historical proximity. Barbara Stewart, writing for <i>The New York Times</i>, once called Therese "the Emily Dickinson of Roman Catholic sainthood".<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, she is the subject of much theological comment and study, and, as a young woman whose message has touched the lives of millions, she remains the focus of much popular devotion.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> She was a highly influential model of sanctity for Catholics in the first half of the twentieth century because of the simplicity and practicality of her approach to the spiritual life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2001399_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2001399-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese was devoted to <a href="/wiki/Eucharistic_Adoration" class="mw-redirect" title="Eucharistic Adoration">Eucharistic adoration</a> and on 26 February 1895, shortly before she died wrote from memory and without a rough draft her poetic masterpiece "<i>To Live by Love</i>" which she had composed during Eucharistic adoration. During her life, the poem was sent to various religious communities and was included in a notebook of her poems.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996245_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996245-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese lived a hidden life and "wanted to be unknown",<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> yet became popular after her death through her spiritual autobiography. She also left letters, poems, religious plays, prayers, and her last conversations were recorded by her sisters. Paintings and photographs&#160;– mostly the work of her sister Céline&#160;– further led to her becoming known.
</p><p>Therese said on her death-bed, "I only love simplicity. I have a horror of pretence", and she spoke out against some of the claims made concerning the lives of saints written in her day, "We should not say improbable things, or things we do not know. We must see their real, and not their imagined lives".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaucher19932_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaucher19932-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The depth of her spirituality, of which she said, "my way is all confidence and love", has inspired <a href="/wiki/List_of_devotees_of_St._Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux" class="mw-redirect" title="List of devotees of St. Thérèse of Lisieux">many believers</a> up to the current day. In the face of her littleness she trusted to God her sanctity. She wanted to go to heaven by an entirely new little way. "I wanted to find an elevator that would raise me to Jesus". The elevator, she wrote, would be the arms of Jesus lifting her in all her littleness.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Holy_Face_of_Jesus_devotion">Holy Face of Jesus devotion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Holy Face of Jesus devotion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg/180px-Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="241" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg/270px-Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg/360px-Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2238" data-file-height="3000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Claude_Mellan_-_Face_of_Christ_-_WGA14764.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A depiction of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Face_of_Jesus" title="Holy Face of Jesus">Holy Face of Jesus</a> as "Veronica's veil", by <a href="/wiki/Claude_Mellan" title="Claude Mellan">Claude Mellan</a> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1649</span></div></div></div>
<p>The devotion to the Holy Face of Jesus was promoted by another Carmelite nun, Sister <a href="/wiki/Marie_of_St_Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="Marie of St Peter">Marie of St Peter</a> in <a href="/wiki/Tours" title="Tours">Tours</a>, France in 1844. Then by <a href="/wiki/Leo_Dupont" title="Leo Dupont">Leo Dupont</a>, also known as the <a href="/wiki/Apostle_of_the_Holy_Face" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostle of the Holy Face">Apostle of the Holy Face</a> who formed the Archconfraternity of the Holy Face in Tours in 1851.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Therese joined this confraternity on April 26, 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Her parents, <a href="/wiki/Marie-Az%C3%A9lie_Gu%C3%A9rin_Martin" class="mw-redirect" title="Marie-Azélie Guérin Martin">Louis and Zélie Martin</a>, had also prayed at the <a href="/wiki/Oratory_of_the_Holy_Face" title="Oratory of the Holy Face">Oratory of the Holy Face</a>, originally established by Dupont in Tours.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> This devotion to the Holy Face of Jesus was based on images of the <a href="/wiki/Veil_of_Veronica" title="Veil of Veronica">Veil of Veronica</a>, as promoted by Dupont, rather than the <a href="/wiki/Shroud_of_Turin" title="Shroud of Turin">Shroud of Turin</a>, which image first appeared on a photographic negative in 1898.
</p><p>On 10 January 1889, she was given the habit and received the name Therese of the Child Jesus. On 8 September 1890, Therese took her vows. The ceremony of "taking the veil" followed on the 24th, when she added to her name in religion <i>of the Holy Face</i>, a title which was to become increasingly important in the development and character of her inner life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres1959164_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres1959164-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> In his <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">"A l'ecole de Therese de Lisieux: maitresse de la vie spirituelle"</i></span>, Bishop Guy Gaucher emphasizes that Therese saw the devotions to the Child Jesus and to the Holy Face as so completely linked that she signed herself "Thérèse de l'Enfant Jesus de la Sainte Face" – Therese of the Child Jesus of the Holy Face. In her poem <i>My Heaven down here</i>, composed in 1895, Therese expressed the notion that by the divine union of love, the soul takes on the semblance of Christ. By contemplating the sufferings associated with the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Face" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Face">Holy Face</a> of Jesus, she felt she could become closer to Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENevin2006184,_228_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENevin2006184,_228-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> She wrote the words "Make me resemble you, Jesus!"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> on a small card and attached a stamp with an image of the Holy Face. She pinned the prayer in a small container over her heart.
</p><p>Therese wrote many prayers to express her devotion to the Holy Face. In August 1895, in her "Canticle to the Holy Face," she wrote: "Jesus, Your ineffable image is the star which guides my steps. Ah, You know, Your sweet Face is for me Heaven on earth. My love discovers the charms of Your Face adorned with tears. I smile through my own tears when I contemplate Your sorrows."
</p><p>Therese emphasised God's mercy in both the birth and the passion narratives in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospel</a>. She wrote, "He sees it disfigured, covered with blood! … unrecognizable! … And yet the divine Child does not tremble; this is what He chooses to show His love".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaForest200061_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaForest200061-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She composed the <i>Holy Face Prayer for Sinners</i>: "Eternal Father, since Thou hast given me for my inheritance the adorable Face of Thy Divine Son, I offer that face to Thee and I beg Thee, in exchange for this coin of infinite value, to forget the ingratitude of souls dedicated to Thee and to pardon all poor sinners."<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Over the decades, her poems and prayers helped to spread the devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Face_of_Jesus" title="Holy Face of Jesus">Holy Face of Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996137_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996137-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg/180px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="265" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg/270px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg/360px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1248" data-file-height="1837" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Martin-Histoire_d%27une_%C3%A2me-A00.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Cover page of <i>The Story of a Soul</i> (<span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">L'Histoire d'une Âme</i></span>) by Therese of Lisieux, édition 1940</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg/220px-Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg/330px-Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg/440px-Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Image-Basilique_de_Lisieux_crytpte.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The crypt of the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_(Lisieux)" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St. Thérèse (Lisieux)">Basilica of St. Therese in Lisieux</a></div></div></div>
<p>Therese is best known today for her spiritual memoir, <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">L'histoire d'une âme</i></span> (<i><a href="/wiki/The_Story_of_a_Soul" class="mw-redirect" title="The Story of a Soul">The Story of a Soul</a></i>). It is a compilation of three separate manuscripts. The first, in 1895 is a memoir of her childhood, written under obedience to the Prioress, Mother Agnes of Jesus, her older sister Pauline. Mother Agnes gave the order after being prompted by their eldest sister, Sister Marie of the Sacred Heart.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The second is a three-page letter, written in September 1896, at the request of her eldest sister Marie, who, aware of the seriousness of Therese's illness, asked her to set down her "little doctrine".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In June 1897, Mother Agnes asked Mother Marie de Gonzague, who had succeeded her as prioress, to allow Therese to write another memoir with more details of her religious life (ostensibly as a help in the later composition of an anticipated obituary).<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While on her deathbed Therese made a number of references to the book's future appeal and benefit to souls. She authorized Pauline to make any changes deemed necessary. It was heavily edited by Pauline (Mother Agnes), who made more than seven thousand revisions to Therese's manuscript and presented it as a biography of her sister. Aside from considerations of style, Mother Marie de Gonzague had ordered Pauline to alter the first two sections of the manuscript to make them appear as if they were addressed to Mother Marie as well.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282_65-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_ThérèseClarke_(tr.)19967–8,_279–282-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The book was sent out as the customary "circular" advising other Carmels of a nun's death and requesting their prayers. However, it received a much wider circulation, as copies were lent out and passed around.
</p><p>Since 1973, two centenary editions of Therese's original, unedited manuscripts, including <i>The Story of a Soul</i>, her letters, poems,<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> prayers and the plays she wrote for the monastery recreations have been published in French. ICS Publications has issued a complete critical edition of her writings: <i>Story of a Soul</i>, <i>Last Conversations</i>, and the two volumes of her letters were translated by John Clarke, O.C.D.; <i>The Poetry of Saint Thérèse</i> by Donald Kinney, O.C.D.; <i>The Prayers of St. Thérèse</i> by Alethea Kane, O.C.D.; and <i>The Religious Plays of St. Thérèse of Lisieux</i> by David Dwyer and Susan Conroy.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Development_of_a_devotion_to_Saint_Therese">Development of a devotion to Saint Therese</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Development of a devotion to Saint Therese">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux,_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery,_Des_Plaines,_Illinois.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg/180px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="473" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg/270px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg/360px-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux%2C_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery%2C_Des_Plaines%2C_Illinois.jpg 2x" data-file-width="678" data-file-height="1781" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux,_statue_at_the_Community_Mausoleum_of_All_Saints_Cemetery,_Des_Plaines,_Illinois.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Therese of Lisieux statue at the Community Mausoleum of <a href="/wiki/All_Saints_Cemetery" title="All Saints Cemetery">All Saints Cemetery</a>, Des Plaines, Illinois</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg/220px-Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg/330px-Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg/440px-Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4100" data-file-height="2986" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Choice_of_250_medals_of_Sainte_Therese.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Sign advertising a choice of 250 medals of Saint Therese on sale in Lisieux</div></div></div>
<p>Céline Martin entered the Lisieux convent on 14 September 1894. With Mother Agnes' permission, she brought her camera to Carmel, and developing materials. "The indulgence was not by any means usual. Also outside of the normal would be the destiny of those photographs Céline would make in the Carmel, images that would be scrutinized and reproduced too many times to count. Even when the images are poorly reproduced, her eyes arrest us. Described as blue, described as gray, they look darker in photographs. Céline's pictures of her sister contributed to the extraordinary cult of personality that formed in the years after Therese's death".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003118_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison2003118-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1902, the Polish <a href="/wiki/Carmelite" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite">Carmelite</a> Father <a href="/wiki/Raphael_Kalinowski" title="Raphael Kalinowski">Raphael Kalinowski</a> translated her autobiography, <i>The Story of a Soul</i>, into Polish.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> As early as 1912 Father Thomas N. Taylor, a teacher at the Diocese of Glasgow seminary, wrote a short hagiography on Thérèse, two years before the case for her canonization would be opened. Taylor went on to become a significant proponent of devotion to "The Little Flower" in Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> As pastor of St. Francis Xavier Church in <a href="/wiki/Carfin" title="Carfin">Carfin</a>, Lanarkshire, he built a replica of the Grotto at Lourdes and included a small shrine honoring Thérèse with a statue donated by the Legion of Mary. Carfin became a site of pilgrimages.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Recognition">Recognition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Recognition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization">Canonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Canonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg/180px-St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg/270px-St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg/360px-St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2017" data-file-height="3280" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Therese_saint_day_17_may_1925.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The canonization of Saint Therese in St. Peter's Basilica, Rome</div></div></div>
<p>The impact of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Story_of_a_Soul" class="mw-redirect" title="The Story of a Soul">The Story of a Soul</a></i>, a collection of her autobiographical manuscripts, printed and distributed a year after her death to an initially very limited audience, was significant. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> made her the "star of his pontificate".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGörres19594_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGörres19594-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Pius X signed the decree for the opening of the process of canonization on 10 June 1914.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XV" title="Pope Benedict XV">Pope Benedict XV</a>, in order to hasten the process, dispensed with the usual fifty-year delay required between death and <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a>. On 14 August 1921, he promulgated the decree on the <a href="/wiki/Heroic_virtue" title="Heroic virtue">heroic virtues</a> of Therese declaring her "Venerable". She was beatified on 29 April 1923.<sup id="cite_ref-Lagoutte_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lagoutte-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese was canonized on 17 May 1925 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a>, only 28 years after her death.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Therese was declared a saint five years and a day after Joan of Arc. However, the 1925 celebration for Therese "far outshone" that for the legendary heroine of France. At the time, Pope Pius XI revived the old custom of covering St. Peter's with torches and tallow lamps. According to one account, "Ropes, lamps and tallows were pulled from the dusty storerooms where they had been packed away for 55 years. A few old workmen who remembered how it was done the last time – in 1870 – directed 300 men for two weeks as they climbed about fastening lamps to St. Peter's dome." <i>The New York Times</i> ran a front-page story about the occasion titled, "All Rome Admires St. Peter's Aglow for a New Saint". According to the <i>Times</i>, over 60,000 people, estimated to be the largest crowd inside St. Peter's Basilica since the coronation of Pope Pius X, 22 years before, witnessed the canonization ceremonies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor1983153_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Connor1983153-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> In the evening, 500,000 pilgrims pressed into the lit square.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996335_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDescouvemont1996335-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She rapidly became one of the most popular saints of the twentieth century. Her feast day was added to the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> in 1927 for celebration on October 3.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> In 1969, 42 years later, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> moved it to October 1, the day after her <i>dies natalis</i> (birthday to heaven).<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Therese of Lisieux is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of aviators, florists, illness(es) and missions. She is also considered by Catholics to be the patron saint of Russia,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> although the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a> does not recognize either her canonization or her patronage. In 1927, Pope Pius XI named Saint Therese co-patron of the missions, with Saint <a href="/wiki/Francis_Xavier" title="Francis Xavier">Francis Xavier</a>. In 1944 Pope Pius XII decreed her a co-patron of France with Saint <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_Arc" title="Joan of Arc">Joan of Arc</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> The principal patron of France is the Blessed Virgin Mary.
</p><p>By the Apostolic Letter <i>Divini Amoris Scientia</i> <i>(The Science of Divine Love)</i> of 19 October 1997, <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> declared her the thirty-third <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> the youngest person, and one of only four women so named, the others being <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hildegard_of_Bingen" title="Hildegard of Bingen">Hildegard of Bingen</a>.
</p><p>Devotion to Therese has developed around the world.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENevin200626_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENevin200626-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> According to some biographies of <a href="/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf" title="Édith Piaf">Édith Piaf</a>, in 1922 the singer – at the time, an unknown seven-year-old girl – was cured from blindness after a pilgrimage to the grave of Therese, who at the time was not yet formally canonized.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_of_her_parents">Canonization of her parents</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Canonization of her parents">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg/180px-Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="270" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg/270px-Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg/360px-Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2912" data-file-height="4368" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_of_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague_-_8151.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Saint Therese of Lisieux at the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lady_Victorious" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of Our Lady Victorious">Church of Our Lady Victorious</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a></div></div></div>
<p>Zélie and Louis Martin were the first spouses to be proposed for canonization as a couple and the first to be canonized together. In 2004, the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Milan" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Milan">Archbishop of Milan</a> accepted the unexpected cure of Pietro Schiliro, an Italian child born near Milan in 2002 with a lung disorder, as a miracle attributable to their intercession. Announced by Cardinal Saraiva Martins on 12 July 2008, at the ceremonies marking the 150th anniversary of the marriage of the Venerable <a href="/wiki/Z%C3%A9lie_Martin" class="mw-redirect" title="Zélie Martin">Zélie</a> and <a href="/wiki/Louis_Martin_(blessed)" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis Martin (blessed)">Louis Martin</a>, their beatification as a couple took place on 19 October 2008,<sup id="cite_ref-Juana_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Juana-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> in Lisieux.
</p><p>In 2011, the letters of Blessed Zélie and Louis Martin were published in English as <i>A Call to a Deeper Love: The Family Correspondence of the Parents of Saint Therese of the Child Jesus, 1863–1885</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-O_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> On 7 January 2013, in <a href="/wiki/Valencia" title="Valencia">Valencia</a>, Spain, the diocesan process opened to examine a "presumed miracle" attributed to their intercession: the healing of a newborn girl, Carmen Pérez Pons, who was born prematurely four days after their beatification and who inexplicably recovered from severe bleeding of the brain and other complications.<sup id="cite_ref-Juana_129-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Juana-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 May 2013, the diocesan process to examine the miracle closed and the dossier was sent to the Congregation for the Causes of Saints in Rome. Louis and Zélie Martin were canonized on 18 October 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Canonization_cause_of_her_sister_L.C3.A9onie"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_cause_of_her_sister_Léonie">Canonization cause of her sister Léonie</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Canonization cause of her sister Léonie">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Therese's older sister, <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9onie_Martin" title="Léonie Martin">Léonie Martin</a>, the only one of the five sisters who did not become a Carmelite nun, is also a candidate for sainthood. Leonie attempted the religious life three times before her fourth and final entrance in 1899 to the convent of the Visitation in Caen. She took the name Sister Françoise-Thérèse and was a fervent disciple of Therese's way. She died in 1941 in <a href="/wiki/Caen" title="Caen">Caen</a>, where her tomb in the crypt of the Visitation Monastery may be visited by the public.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> On 25 March 2012, Mgr Jean-Claude Boulanger, Bishop of Bayeux and Lisieux, granted the imprimatur for a prayer asking that Leonie might be declared venerable.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> On 2 July 2015, the diocesan inquiry into Leonie's life and possible sanctity was opened at the chapel of the Monastery of the Visitation at Caen. She is now styled <i>Leonie Martin, <a href="/wiki/Servant_of_God" title="Servant of God">Servant of God</a></i>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics_of_Saint_Therese">Relics of Saint Therese</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Relics of Saint Therese">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg/220px-Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="219" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg/330px-Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg/440px-Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2631" data-file-height="2621" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Close_up_of_First_Class_Relic_of_Saint_Therese_of_Lisieux.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Detail of a first-class carne <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relic</a> of Saint Therese of Lisieux</div></div></div><p>The relics of Saint Therese have been on an international pilgrimage since 1994.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The tour included not only first-class relics, but also the saint's religious habit, her rosary, and several other items. They were brought to Ireland in the summer of 2001.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> That same year they travelled to Canada. Although Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Basil_Hume" title="Basil Hume">Basil Hume</a> had declined to endorse proposals for a tour in 1997, her relics finally visited England and Wales in late September and early October 2009, including an overnight stop at the <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> <a href="/wiki/York_Minster" title="York Minster">York Minster</a> on her feast day, 1 October. A quarter of a million people venerated them.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ronald_J._Garan_Jr." title="Ronald J. Garan Jr.">Ron Garan</a>, who was on the May 31–June 14 <i><a href="/wiki/Space_Shuttle_Discovery" title="Space Shuttle Discovery">Discovery</a></i> shuttle mission in 2008, took a relic of Saint Therese with him, which had been given to him by the Carmelites of New Caney, Texas. The Carmelites-based this on the wish of Therese "to preach the Gospel on all five continents simultaneously and even to the most remote isles."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 June 2010, relics of Saint Therese went on their first visit to South Africa in conjunction with the <a href="/wiki/2010_FIFA_World_Cup" title="2010 FIFA World Cup">2010 FIFA World Cup</a>. They remained in the country until October 5, 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The writing-desk Therese used at Carmel (an artifact as opposed to a relic) toured the United States in September and October 2013, sponsored by the Pontifical Mission Societies in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2013, a new reliquary containing the relics of Saint Therese and of her parents, was presented to the Archdiocese of Philadelphia by the Magnificat Foundation.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> It was first exposed for veneration at the Magnificat Day on 9 November 2013.
</p><p>The National Shrine of St. Therese in Darien, Illinois, has the largest collection of relics and personal artifacts of the saint outside of Lisieux.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Episcopal_Veneration">Episcopal Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Episcopal Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 2022, Thérèse was officially added to the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church liturgical calendar</a> with a feast day on 1 October.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg/220px-Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg/330px-Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg/440px-Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6655" data-file-height="4437" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Basilique_Sainte-Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux-2876.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>With more than two million visitors a year, the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se,_Lisieux" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St. Thérèse, Lisieux">Basilica of St. Thérèse in Lisieux</a> is the second-largest pilgrimage site in France, after <a href="/wiki/Lourdes" title="Lourdes">Lourdes</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_(Lisieux)" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St. Thérèse (Lisieux)">Basilica of St. Thérèse</a> in her home town of Lisieux was consecrated on 11 July 1954 and has become a centre for pilgrims from all over the world. It was originally dedicated in 1937 by Cardinal Eugenio Pacelli, later <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>.
